37:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","em",null,{"children":"Note: This content section largely focuses on the steganography component of this module. For the content on backdoors, see \"Learn more\" and the teaching guide that outlines how this material was taught in-person."}]}]

39:["$","h3",null,{"id":"41-introduction-to-steganography","children":"4.1 Introduction to steganography"}]
3a:["$","p",null,{"children":"Steganography is the art and science of hidden communication. Unlike cryptography, which protects the content of a message by making it unreadable to outsiders, steganography seeks to conceal the very existence of the message itself. A secret note may be embedded in an innocent-looking image, a line of text, a sound file, or even a network protocol, so that an observer sees only ordinary communication. At its core, steganography asks a subtle question: how can information be transmitted without revealing that communication is taking place at all?"}]
3b:["$","p",null,{"children":"A steganographic setup typically consists of:"}]
3c:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"A sender and a receiver"}],", often called the encoder and decoder, who share a secret key."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"A communication channel"}],", through which messages are transmitted."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"A distribution of innocent messages"}],", describing what ordinary communication over the channel looks like."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"A message space"}],", containing the secret messages that the sender may wish to transmit."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"An encoding procedure"}],", which embeds a secret message into an apparently innocent message."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"A decoding procedure"}],", which recovers the secret message from the received communication."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"A correctness condition"}],", requiring that decoding succeeds with high probability."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"An adversary"}],", or warden, who observes the communication and tries to distinguish ordinary messages from steganographic ones."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"A security condition"}],", requiring that the adversary's distinguishing advantage is small."]}],"\n"]}]
3d:["$","h3",null,{"id":"42-the-stego-game","children":"4.2 The Stego Game"}]
3e:["$","p",null,{"children":"We start the day with a little game that practically explores the ideas of steganography. We need: at least 4 students, 2 rooms, pen, paper and a blackboard."}]
3f:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Strategy Phase:"}],"\n"]}]

4c:["$","h3",null,{"id":"43-perfect-steganography-vs-computational-steganography","children":"4.3 Perfect Steganography vs Computational Steganography"}]
4d:["$","p",null,{"children":"Perfect steganography, or information-theoretic steganography, means that without the secret key, the attacker doesn't have enough information to retrieve the secret payload message, no matter how much computational power they have. We included here a short proof of the entropy of the message being lower-bounded by the entropy of the key. This explains why perfect security would be cumbersome in practice, as it needs the key length to scale linearly with message length."}]
4e:["$","p",null,{"children":["A short shared key cannot give unbounded perfect IT indistinguishability, so one typically settles for ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"computational indistinguishability"}],". Under standard cryptographic assumptions such as the existence of ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"one-way functions"}]," (hence Pseudo-Random Functions), any ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"efficient"}]," attacker cannot distinguish the stego-text from the innocent one except with negligible advantage. This does ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"not"}]," protect against an attacker with unlimited computation, but the generic attack is exhaustive search over the key space, so the effort is at most about ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"2^{\\lambda}","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"<span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"base\"><span class=\"strut\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord\">2</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal mtight\">λ</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>"}}],", and security aims to be of that order."]}]
